# Changelog

Most changelogs are ledgers of outcomes: Problem X → Bug Y → Solution Z — what changed, with the reasoning thrown
away. This one keeps the reasoning. Each row records what was believed, what was measured, which belief did not
survive the measurement, who caught it, and why the fix is structural rather than a patch. Wrong claims are
corrected in place and kept — a ledger that quietly edits itself is worth nothing, and several rows below exist
only because an earlier row was wrong.

The direction of this project is also reversed, and the log reads accordingly. Most protocols travel idea →
specification → hoped-for adoption. UST traveled the other way: a working operator — noosphere.md — ran first, and
the protocol was extracted from what production kept proving necessary; then formalized against a mathematical
model; then widened so that no operator, including ours, is special — a core any implementation can conform to,
connectors for everything else (anchor substrates, trackers, transports). The rows below are that extraction, one
adversarial round at a time.

The wire format `ust:"1.0"` is **stable across every release candidate**, so a document sealed by any of them
verifies under any other. Pin exact package versions for reproducibility — and **watch the registry while you do**.
On 2026-07-28 a consumer was measured twenty candidates behind with its own declared range permitting the newer
one the whole time; only a lockfile held it, and the capability it lacked was the one that lifts a stream verdict
off `chain-consistent`. A stable wire makes upgrading safe; it does not make standing still free.

This log tracks version lines newest-first, the **reference checker (L1 TCB)** rev-ladder — a recurring
diverse-model adversarial audit → math-first remediation — the **milestones**, and the **release policy** an
outsider can hold us to. The normative source is the git history plus the conformance vectors; this file is the
readable map.

## rc.73 line — unpublished

rc.72 reached npm, and round 239 changed `index.mjs` — the signature faculty is no longer bound to one caller. A
published artifact is immutable, so the tree moves to rc.73 rather than re-cutting what consumers already hold.
The line is opened by that PUBLICATION, exactly as the rule below states; the round is its first row.

## rc.72 line

The core moved after rc.70 published, so the version moved with it: a published artifact is immutable, and
`REGISTRY.partitionKinds` changed `index.mjs` (round 206). It moved again for the same structural reason —
`REGISTRY.anchorRefusalReasons` (round 207) — so the tree carries rc.72 while `latest` on npm is still rc.70.

**A line is opened by a PUBLICATION, never by a round.** rc.71 never reached npm, so it never earned a line of
its own; both rounds are rows of THIS line and publish together. Their version cells record what the tree carried
when each round closed.

## rc.70 line

The repository is AHEAD of npm again: the inclusion seam closed end to end after rc.69 shipped, so the
producer, the core and the connector all moved. Rounds 205 and up are here.

## rc.69 line

The repository is AHEAD of npm and says so here, where an outsider reads it. `cannotDecide` (#144, round 189)
exists in this tree and not in what `npm i` installs; the line publishes when #144 closes, because its remaining
half — the asynchronous byte-admitted surface — is a breaking change to the public API and belongs in the same
release as the fix, not one release behind it.

| **rc.73** | 269 | **The repository wrote down the guarantee and never enforced it: the lockfile's account of our OWN packages (#179).** `root-inventory-gate` states why the file is tracked — *`npm ci` needs it, and what a stranger installs is what CI tested is otherwise an intention (#87)* — and nothing read the record back. Refreshing the lock to answer a Dependabot alert moved NINE entries and only ONE was the dependency being patched: eight were ours, recording versions their own manifests had already passed, `ust-cli` rc.106 against rc.108 and `ust-mcp` rc.61 against rc.63 among them. **Why nothing caught it, measured in both directions rather than assumed:** a dependency-range desync makes `npm ci` REFUSE (EUSAGE), so that axis is covered by npm and this gate does not duplicate it — while a stale workspace VERSION makes `npm ci` exit 0, and no gate in this tree ever read that field. `npm-drift-check` guards the opposite direction (repo bytes against an already-published tarball) and never opens the lock; the NAME is what carried the assumption of coverage. **Severity stated honestly, because the first draft of this note overstated it:** a workspace installs as a SYMLINK, so CI always ran the repo's bytes and no gate ever tested stale code. What was wrong is the RECORD — a tracked, published artifact misreporting the versions of the packages it belongs to. The new gate enumerates (declared workspace × lock entry) in BOTH directions, since a package the lock forgot and an entry no workspace claims are one defect seen from either end. Proven against the real historical state: 9 failures on the pre-fix lock, 0 after. Third round running in which the finding was a rule already written and never executed. |
| **rc.73** | 268 | **A supply remedy must be capable of being true for THIS document, and F.5.1d had stated that condition only for the CALL (#179).** `explainLadder` handed a key-form document the name-form hint BYTE FOR BYTE — *publish `/.well-known/ust-genesis` … every consumer gains the same* — for a shard that IS the key and has no name to serve under. Following it is worse than inert: the verdict is identical with and without the genesis, and supplying it moves the identity coordinate's `status` from `verified` to `unavailable` with `authority pending, retry`, pointing the publisher at no-fork evidence for a name it never claimed. **The mechanical reason it survived: `absentInputs` read the CALL only, so a clause quantifying over `d` was derived from `o` and had no access to the fact that refutes it.** **And the rule already existed with no executor.** F.5.1c forbids collapsing settled-absent into not-brought; the vector `ladder-05-key-form-identity-is-nobodys-to-raise` cites it and its own text says the report must not list one as if it would — while its expectations checked three neighbouring facts and never the listing its sentence forbids. Model stated, vector named, suite green, implementation doing the opposite. Worse, all seven ladder vectors ran against the SAME key-form document (the corpus builder's default shard), so the settled case was pinned seven times and the live case never. F.5.1g states the dual: `∀ d, o, i ∈ R(d, o). ∃ o′ ⊇ o, i ∈ dom(o′) : π(𝓡(d, o′)) ≠ π(𝓡(d, o))`. Settled entries carry `settled: true`, `movable: false` and a hint that REFUSES rather than instructs; a ladder vector now names the document FORM it is about; `ladder-08` is the control without which the corollary is satisfied by settling everything. Three mutations, each reddening the check that states the claim rather than a neighbour. |

| **rc.73** | 261 | **The key-holder split, built — and the seam turned out to be CHECKABLE without a key (#178).** `encryptPartition` derives what must be sealed and then seals it; only the second half needs a key, and a key passed as a tool argument travels through a model's context into every transcript recording it. `sealingRequest` returns the commitment, the exact plaintext to seal and the IV — all key-free — and `attachEncryption` assembles the block a holder returns. **Measured: the split's output is BYTE-IDENTICAL to `encryptPartition`'s**, which is what makes F.7a.2's producer corollary true across a process boundary — it demands one DERIVATION, not one process. **And the seam is not merely trusted.** The IV is `H('ust:enc-iv', commit)[0..width]`, so the ciphertext's nonce prefix is determined by a commitment any key-free party can recompute: a sealer working from its own derivation is caught by a caller holding no key at all (`E-COMMIT`). What cannot be checked there is the AEAD binding, which needs the key and is §14 step 8's job. | **rc.73** | 258 | **`seal` was two operations under one name, and the half that needs no key came back inside the protocol surface (#178). `@ust-protocol/web-signer@1.0.0-rc.27`.** The agent's most common publication path ran AROUND the protocol: `signing_input` went out, the agent signed with its own key, and it assembled `{ust,state,sig}` by hand — so nowhere in the flow was *the parts you put together actually verify* ever checked. An agent that skipped its own verification published documents every reader refuses and heard about it from the reader, or never. Three hand-written assemblers existed in this tree, each with its own way to mismatch `pub` and `key_id`; `ust-web-signer` took `key_id` from the STATE while `pub` came from the SIGNER, so a state built for one identity and signed by another failed E-SIG at a reader and nowhere earlier. `attachSignature` DERIVES `key_id` from `pub` — §12.2 defines it as a hash of the key, so asking a caller to restate it can only produce disagreement — and VERIFIES before returning, refusing to hand back a document a reader would reject. Exposed as `ust_seal`; the private key still never travels, which was never the gap. **Third occurrence of one shape across these two cards**: `disclosure` (produce vs consume), `build-transcript` (state vs ceremonies), now `sign` (sign vs assemble). Debt ratchet 13 → 12. |
| **rc.73** | 257 | **Agents can make a `blinded` partition now, and the envelope comes back in the SAME object as the state (#178).** The mode the principal audience needs most — publish existence, time and integrity WITHOUT the value, proving what you did without disclosing what it was — needs no key at all, so the sentence that kept it off this surface was true of `encrypted` and false here. Privacy is declared PER PARTITION in the data, exactly as the wire carries it and as `ust sign` takes it, across all three builders: a derivation's partitions are no less private than an observation's. **The envelope is returned WITH the state rather than on request**, which is the structural form of an obligation the CLI can only state in a flag — §10's nonce is generated here, so a tool handing back the state alone would leave the agent holding a commitment nobody can ever open, itself included. An agent cannot receive one without the other, so the obligation cannot be declined. `encrypted` is refused with a claim about the ACT and not about agents: the key would travel as a tool argument, hence through the model's context and into every transcript recording it. Debt ratchet 14 → 13. |
| **rc.73** | 256 | **Every absence on the agent surface is now CLASSIFIED, and the debt is pinned (#178).** The agent is the protocol's principal audience, and its surface is the poorer of the two — 18 capabilities reachable from the CLI against 12 from MCP. A human at a terminal has a shell and the package, so a missing tool costs them six lines; an agent has exactly what is exposed, so the same absence is an inconvenience on one surface and a wall on the other. The 20 MCP absences are split by the owner's test — *would this still need a human if we trusted the agent completely?* — into **6 deferred** (the requirement is a property of the ACT: minting an identity, re-rooting a chain, closing a key log) and **14 lagging** (debt). The two are argued in different registers ON PURPOSE: a deferred cell owes a claim about the act, checkable by reading it; a lagging cell owes NOTHING, and the gate now REFUSES a justification written under one — a rationale on a debt is absence wearing the vocabulary of intent, which is exactly how `disclosure-produce` sat unowned for two months behind a sentence defending a risk that was not present. The lagging count is a ratchet: a capability landing on the CLI and not on MCP raises it and fails the build. Verified by three mutations — dropping a classification, rationalising a debt, and deferring without an argument each redden their own message. |
| **rc.73** | 255 | **The floor could refuse a private partition and not make one — and the corpus leg that would have said so did not exist (#177). `ust-light@1.0.0-rc.58`.** `ust-light` validated the `enc` SHAPE, had no producer and no §14 step 8, so every fixture it could hold described its own verifier. It now BUILDS both modes at the MTI (`blindPartition`, `encryptPartition` over WebCrypto AES-GCM, IV derived from the commitment so uniqueness reduces to what §10 already demands) and READS a disclosure per-channel — and what it writes, the full reference verifier opens. XChaCha stays this floor's honest ceiling: WebCrypto has no ChaCha, and a hand-rolled cipher is the one thing this package refuses. **Then the corpus leg found four more, all pre-existing.** Its test read SIX `class-role` vectors and nothing else, so a population that looked like coverage was one slice: the closed private production, the identifier restriction and the blinded-carrying-a-ciphertext refusal were all ABSENT here after both other implementations had them — and `kind:"absence"` was missing outright, so an honest document of the reference operator was REFUSED at the floor. That is #154 recurring in the implementation that round did not sweep. §13's structural breadth bound was absent too, in the other direction: the floor accepted what the core refuses. All five closed; the leg now compares every document-bearing vector, auxiliary inputs included. |
| **rc.73** | 254 | **`SUPPORT.md` — what each surface actually does, on one page, RENDERED rather than written (#177).** A hand-kept support table is a promise, and this repository has already paid for one: the capability map said `ust-cli` did `sign` because the word `seal` occurred inside ceremony internals. So the page is generated from the same declarations `capability-parity` probes against live surfaces on every run — a cell is true because the gate would go red otherwise, and CI diffs the rendered file. **It contradicted itself on the first render, which is the argument for generating it:** the grid showed `disclosure` absent from every surface while the prose beneath said the core and the CLI make private partitions. The capability's blanket reason — *a publisher-side act on data the tool never holds* — covered TWO operations and was true of one: a verifier never holds the pair, a PUBLISHER holds exactly what `encryptPartition` takes. Renamed `disclosure-produce` and owned by the CLI; consumption is `verify` with `disclosures`/`decKeys` and is scored there. Also corrected by the same render: `sign` is now FULL for the CLI and `build-transcript` SUBSET — `ust sign` builds a state and the ceremonies a genesis/key-log, while nothing there builds an attestation, a checkpoint, a gap or an anchor commitment, and calling that full would repeat the overclaim round 246 removed. |
| **rc.73** | 253 | **`ust sign` — the CLI can finally turn your own data into a signed transcript, and the clean-room verifier gained the §14 step 8 it never had (#177).** Fifteen commands and none of them signed a document: a publisher wrote JavaScript, always. Privacy is declared PER PARTITION in the input, as the wire carries it, so the command is a translator rather than a policy — and `--disclosures-out` is MANDATORY the moment a partition is private, because the nonce is generated here and a tool that keeps it leaves the publisher holding a commitment nobody can ever open. It refuses to invent an AEAD key too (§10 leaves key management to the operator), and it verifies what it WROTE — re-opening every private partition from the envelope before handing either over. **Found by cross-checking its output:** `docs/ust-verify.mjs` had ZERO occurrences of `disclosures` — no step 8 at all — so an authorized reader passing a WRONG `{nonce,value}` got `VALID` from it, and the commitment binding that is the entire point of §10 was unenforced on that surface. It now reproduces the commitment and, through WebCrypto, opens AES-GCM. **And the gate that should have seen it was itself inert:** `docs-verifier-parity` called both verifiers with `{context}` alone, so every check that only acts when a disclosure is supplied was compared in a configuration where it could not act. Vectors now carry their auxiliary inputs into both, and `INDETERMINATE(unsupported_alg)` is separated from disagreement — a build reporting its own limit is declining to answer, not diverging (the clean-room has no ChaCha). |

But six was arbitrary, and an arbitrary constant is only as trustworthy as the comment beside it. Owner: `commitment` and `ciphertext` are both ten characters. The run is now `label.length`, so the count is a property of OUR vocabulary and provably cannot encode anything about the value — and a third channel gets its own width for free instead of inheriting a number nobody re-derived. |
| **rc.73** | 251 | **The web verifier could not open a private partition at all, and printed a hash where a reader needed a state (#177).** The page already takes an offline genesis and key log — auxiliary inputs a reader supplies, none of which leave the browser — and a disclosure is the same class of thing; it had no field for one. It now takes the `{nonce,value}` envelope and the key map, spread into ALL THREE call paths rather than copied into one (the CLI lost exactly these by hand-listing its options into one of two paths, round 247). A private partition also gains the `md ⇄ raw` toggle a public one always had: **raw** is the envelope on the wire, **md** draws the two channels §14.8 decides — commitment and ciphertext — each either carrying its value or sealed. The seal is a FIXED run of six glyphs, and the fixity is the point: a run whose length tracked the hidden value would leak the one thing §10 exists to withhold. Two glyphs, because the modes hide differently — `#` for `blinded` (the value is behind a hash) and `▮` for `encrypted` (opaque bytes). Found while building it: `.sealed` named both the seal run and the state chip, so the chip inherited the run's letter-spacing and opacity — caught by reading the rendered page, not the source. |
| **rc.73** | 250 | **The envelope is a PRODUCT and we were sampling it; enumerating it found a third exploit on its first run (#177).** Round 249 closed the `blinded`-carrying-`enc` hole with a vector, but a vector pins ONE case and that defect was not a wrong case — it was an UNCONSIDERED one, in a corpus with good coverage of every case anyone had thought of. So the probe that found it became a GATE: every cell of (kind × privacy × foreign-member) is rebuilt, signed and verified on each run, with the bases read from `REGISTRY.partitionKinds` so a fourth kind enters the product the day it is registered rather than the day somebody writes vectors for its combinations. **And it asks BOTH implementations**, which is what earned its `1b` grade after the assurance map refused the claim on one — on the first cross-implementation run the clean-room web verifier disagreed on **39 of 42 cells**: it had no closed-envelope check at all. The worst was a `blinded` partition carrying a plaintext `value` contradicting its own commitment, `VALID:LIGHT` on the page a stranger visits, where a consumer reading `part.value` gets the value the commitment does NOT bind. Closed in `docs/ust-verify.mjs` and its byte-identical extension copy. 95 assertions, 42/42 foreign members refused by both. |
| **rc.73** | 249 | **Attacked rounds 243–248 and found two working exploits, one of them mine (#177).** **(a) A `blinded` partition may not carry a ciphertext.** §4.4 wrote the two private alternatives as ONE production with an unconditionally-optional `enc`, while the verifier's AEAD branch is keyed on the MODE — so a document declaring `blinded` and shipping an `enc` block verified `VALID:LIGHT` with the partition reported fully disclosed, and a key-holder decrypting out of band recovered `{reading:"9"}` where the commitment binds `{reading:"5"}`. Two values fixed at one instant with the record accountable for one. The productions are split; the shape is refused at admission. A systematic probe of every (kind × privacy × foreign-field) cell found this was the ONLY hole in the envelope schema — 18 of 19 were already closed. **(b) An identifier the verdict quotes may not forge structure.** `enc.key_id` and a private partition NAME travel INTO the verdict, so a publisher writes part of what the reader sees; neither was constrained beyond `typeof === 'string'`. A newline in either made the reference CLI print a fabricated `tier : [TOP] anchored in Bitcoin, block 964576` line above the real `[LIGHT]` one, out of its own report. §4.3a had taken exactly this disposition for `domain_shard` — a homograph deceiving *a human reading the verdict* — and the rule was never extended to the class. Identifiers are constrained at admission; the 37 sites that quote document strings into a `detail` are covered by ONE mechanism inside `bad()`, so the thirty-eighth is safe the day it is written. VALUES stay exempt: content is the renderer's to escape. Both fixes had to be written TWICE — `docs/ust-verify.mjs` is a clean-room second verifier and the parity gate caught it still admitting all three attacks after the core was safe; a third copy ships in the extension and must stay byte-identical. |

| version | round | what closed |

| version | round | what closed |

| **rc.66**<br>**cli rc.99**<br>**mcp rc.55**<br>**operator 0.10.2**<br>**diarium 0.2.21** | 148 | **Head-recovery is the FRAME EVENT, not the repair of one field (F.5r-g.2).** Found by the gap-record drill on the reference operator, which is what fault injection is for — it did not find what it was aimed at. Adopting a published document is the stream ACCEPTING it, so by F.5r-d it moves one GROUP: head, count, interval bound. `recoverHead` moved the head alone. **The halves fail differently:** an under-counted stream under-claims, which F.5r-d already establishes as the safe direction, but a stale interval bound is UNSAFE — §9 requires a stream checkpoint's `to` to be the last frame's `ust_id`, so an hour sealed after a partial head-recovery would claim bounds that do not bound its own set, and a consumer answers `E-PREV`. **Measured:** head-recovery moved the head, and the first reader of the pair — a gap backfill, which measures a hole FROM the interval bound — found a document occupying the slot where the bound said the next one began, and correctly refused to declare a gap over it. The document was the operator's own, the one the head had just adopted. The defect was invisible to every check that read the head, because the head was RIGHT; it surfaced only where two members of the group were read together. That is the general shape worth keeping: a group split across writers is detected by whoever reads the pair, and until someone does, each half looks correct. The fix is the same door, not a second one — `recordFrame`, exactly what an ordinary append uses. |
| **rc.66**<br>**cli rc.99**<br>**mcp rc.55**<br>**operator 0.10.2**<br>**diarium 0.2.21** | 147 | **A predicate has a DOMAIN on which it is non-trivial, and key-form is where BINDING is not one (F.5w).** An upward correction, not new work: round 145 recorded its math layer as *"no new rule — three existing rules a copy never received"*, and that was wrong about one thing. The round had also shown WHY a green battery proved nothing, and that reason is a theorem. `key-form ⇒ domain_shard = key_id`, so the authority a stream names IS the frame's own key, and the impersonation guard `K_A(key_id) = sig.pub` reduces to `pub = pub` — an IDENTITY on that sub-domain. Vacuity here is not weakness but total absence of discrimination: a suite drawn only from key-form cannot detect the guard being removed, inverted, or never written. Measured, all three were true at once — every hand-written stream case was key-form, the clean-room verifier had no binding at all, and an impostor's frames on a VICTIM's genesis graded a `complete` stream under the victim's name, with the gate green throughout and honest, because it never asked a question that has an answer. **The general form is the one to keep:** for a predicate `P`, a conformance set that never intersects `dom(P)` returns the same verdict whether `P` exists or not, so coverage is argued against `dom(P)` and `|S|` is not evidence — the same statement the day's other two failures made at other layers, and sharpest here because the untested region is defined by a MODE the protocol itself makes trivially satisfying. The realization is already shipped (round 145 moved both parity gates onto the corpus, whose stream vectors are name-form, so `dom(Bound)` is entered by construction); round 145's ladder record is corrected in place to point here rather than rewritten, so the lag stays visible. 814 checks, 159 vectors. |
| **rc.66**<br>**cli rc.99**<br>**mcp rc.55**<br>**operator 0.10.2**<br>**diarium 0.2.21** | 146 | **A declared absence is the STRONGEST statement that nothing was observed — so it may never back a no-event claim (F.5v).** Found while preparing the reference operator to print its first gap records, by asking the protocol question before writing the operator code. Two mechanisms, each sound alone: a signed gap record makes an interval `complete`, and a `complete` interval plus observational coverage grades a no-event claim `completeness-backed`. Composed, they paid the publisher for its own blindness — **a window whose slots were covered ONLY by gap records graded `completeness-backed`**, which is defined as *every covered slot positively observed*, about slots where the publisher had signed a statement that it produced no frame at all. The record exists to turn an unexplained hole into a declared one; uncorrected, the more honestly a publisher confessed an outage, the stronger its *"nothing happened"* became. **The implementation followed a LIST where the rule stood beside it:** the normative text named one blind case (`kind:"absence"`/`reason:"unreachable"`) and the code accepted any `computed` partition — and `data.gap` is one. So the enumeration is now of the POSITIVE set, which is closed and small, with everything else blind by default. The predicate is CLASS-FIRST, because the blindness is not a property of a partition name: an attestation speaks about documents, an observation about the world, so no attestation contributes an observation whatever it carries. **And the fix's own first version was fail-OPEN** — it asked *"does this frame cover a slot?"* and skipped whatever it did not recognise, so an unrecognised class fell out of the question and read as harmless. The existing blind-slot check caught it within a minute. The question here is *"was this window safely observed?"*, so only a POSITIVELY identified non-covering shape is excused — an attestation that is not a gap record, i.e. a stream checkpoint or a batch commitment — and everything else must answer. Three vectors pin the domain in both directions, including the mirror that keeps the rule from being satisfied by refusing everything: a checkpoint inside the window covers no grid slot, is never asked, and must not flip a legitimate backing. 814 checks, 159 vectors. |

Six of the seven predate round 144. **The generator was the gate, not the verifier:** parity was a hand-written battery of 13 cases, and its own comment already records this failing once — *"a battery that does not name a class cannot detect a divergence in it — enumerate the DOMAIN of classes, not a sample"* — after which four more hand-written rows were added, which names four more instances. | **rc.66**<br>**cli rc.99**<br>**mcp rc.55**<br>**operator 0.10.2**<br>**diarium 0.2.21** | 144 | **A root is published TWICE, and only one of the two publications can enumerate (F.5u).** A `set` attestation is a SEAL: it names its members and its root is recomputable from them. The root an operator commits to a public log is a different assertion — *"I committed this root to this substrate in this window"* — and until now the protocol gave it no document form, because `root` was FORBIDDEN on a prev-only attestation and REQUIRED to come with `constituents` everywhere else. So an operator holding a batch root had exactly two conforming moves: enumerate what is under it, or say nothing. **Enumeration is neither necessary nor sufficient.** It is not an input to the §11.2 inclusion predicate — a consumer walking a path from a document to a root never reads a member list — and as an audit it proves only the publisher's arithmetic against a list the publisher chose, since learning the list is the real one means enumerating the stream anyway. Meanwhile it DISCLOSES: a published member list is a membership ORACLE over the batch, letting anyone holding a candidate document test whether it was there, a capability that otherwise belongs only to a holder of the path. An operator batching for many principals therefore cannot enumerate at all, and the requirement drives it outside the protocol. **Measured on the reference operator, which is exactly where it went:** its hourly anchor is an unsigned JSON blob wearing `"protocol": "UST"` — 956 of them — while the conforming path would have cost **3 documents per hour at a 30 s cadence and 58 at the finest grid `ust_id` addresses**, two node attestations and a root, for an object whose entire content is one hash. `root` now FOLLOWS the subtype rather than deciding it: required for `set` and for the new `anchor` subtype, forbidden for `checkpoint` and `gap`, and the subtype remains the NAMED DATA PARTITION it has been since C2. The window travels as `from`/`to` in `ust_id` terms, so an hourly, a daily and a per-second anchorer publish the same document instead of each baking its granularity into a field name. **Two things the model forces rather than chooses:** a commitment can never appear among its own constituents — its `content_hash` depends on its `root`, so membership would be a hash cycle — which is why its own anchored time comes from a strictly later batch and why its order must be carried by `prev`; and a size claim inside a commitment is refutable only DOWNWARD (produce one more path than claimed) and never upward, so it binds an honest publisher and not a dishonest one and may not be a verification input. That last one is the same field name that IS safe in a stream checkpoint — because the safety was never in the field, it was in the enumerable grid the checkpoint is measured against. The subtype matrix ships as the full domain: 3 subtypes × root present/absent, plus a missing `prev`, plus every pair, plus none. **Mutation-tested, and it earned it — two of the first eight vectors were green while asserting nothing:** the no-subtype case was being rejected by the bijection rule for an empty `data`, and the exactly-one rule was enforced by no vector at all. 809 checks, 156 vectors. |

Two instances behind a balancer, two regions, a deploy overlapping its predecessor: each consumer is served a locally consistent chain, every verdict is correct on the evidence seen, and the fork is undetected everywhere. Detection downstream is a POSSIBILITY; prevention upstream is a GUARANTEE. So `Stream` takes a **store port** (`memoryStore()` remains the default, so short jobs are untouched), `STREAM_KEYS` names the state as the LAYER's contract rather than each operator's invention, `resumeFromStore()` continues the same chain in another process, and `append` REFUSES with `E-FORK` when the stored head is not the one this instance last wrote. **And the guarantee is named honestly:** `get`/`set` yields `detected`, compare-and-set yields `prevented`, and the layer never claims the stronger one — the same discipline as not reporting an unattestable property (F.5o) or a universal claim over an undeclared domain (F.5q). Five checks and a language-neutral `depth-law` vector carry it to a port. **And the class check the issue asked for exists now**: `tools/worked-example-gate.mjs`. Every fenced `json` block in the specification must declare what it is — a SKELETON with visible placeholders, or a WORKED EXAMPLE marked `<!-- ust:worked-example -->` and VERIFIED against the reference checker. The teeth are the third case: a block that parses, carries no placeholder and bears no marker FAILS, which is the §21.2 shape exactly — something reading like a transcript that nobody ever ran. Measured: 6 blocks, all skeletons, zero worked examples, so the verify leg has an empty domain today and a control proves it can still fail. Injecting a complete-looking unmarked block is rejected by name. CI 65 → 66 steps. |

So every optional surface was judged on observation alone, and observation collapses two different facts into one `absent`: *this operator does not run that surface* (settled — unattestable now and later) and *it exists and did not answer* (unknown — attestable tomorrow). Both rendered as one `skip`, which is not caution, it is discarding a distinction. **F.5p** makes the verdict a function of the PAIR `(declared, observed)`: declared+absent FAILS as a promise not kept, undeclared+absent is NOT OFFERED, and present attests either way. Two properties make it admissible under the never-self-declared invariant — declaring is **monotone in obligation** (it can only turn a skip into a failure, never into a pass), and the present row never consults the declaration, so silence cannot hide evidence. **And the profile turned out to carry a latent contradiction:** §20 listed "key-log location" among the operator's choices while §12.1/§20.1 fix it at a well-known path. A verifier resolving there would root authority in bytes served by the host whose authority is in question — Theorem F.5p forbids it, the clause is removed, and `surfaceVerdict` REFUSES a relocated surface (`E-DISCOVERY`) rather than ranking it lower: a relocated root is not weaker evidence, it is not evidence. Nothing read the profile, so this was latent rather than live, and it is corrected in the same change that first gives the profile a reader. Four `discovery-surface` vectors, six checks (772 total), and the CLI now reads the profile and defers what an absent surface MEANS to the core. |

A publisher could run the ceremony, hold a signed, self-verifying genesis, publish it, and **no consumer could ever resolve a key from it.** Dead identity, fail-closed, discovered only by whoever tried to use it. **And the ceremony's own self-check could not catch it**, because it asserted the two documents VERIFY — which is the shadow of the property, not the property. It now requires the pair to RESOLVE, with the root and the operational key both active. Swept: only `ust-protocol` and `ust-cli` build a genesis, and the CLI calls the core's builder rather than a copy, so one predicate closes every surface — `web-signer` never had one. **The either/or is gone: the spec chose, not me.** Also in this line — the whole reason it was found: **#115's central claim was FALSE and it was my measurement.** I reported six failed attempts to mint a chain; five of them were `Object.keys()` on a `Map`, which is always zero. The chain mints from the documented exports, and `new_key_id` is not required either. The page now carries a second `runnable:` example that mints genesis + key-log and reaches **VALID:HIGH**, executed by the gate — the honest closing evidence, since an example that runs cannot be a claim I mismeasured. **Where this came from, stated because the rule now says it must be:** none of it was found by a gate here. published tarball, not as pixels — stated rather than glossed.

The guard written five rounds ago is what made the difference between it and the three that fell, which is the argument for those legs stated as evidence rather than as belief. |

## rc.41 line

Opened because rc.40 is PUBLISHED and a published version is immutable: this line registers three canonical
string sets in the core, which changes `index.mjs`, so the core needed a new number before the change could land.

**PUBLISHED 2026-07-30** — `ust-protocol@1.0.0-rc.41`, and not on a schedule: `version-truth` refused the tree.
A new export in the repo and absent from npm is a capability gap pinned at ZERO, and that gate's own text says
*publish, or state the policy — do not raise the number*. So the line shipped when the ratchet said it must, which
is the ratchet doing exactly the job it was pinned for. It carries: the three canonical string sets measured
against code usage (round 86), §15's outcome kinds restored to the verdict slot and `kind: 'fork-choice'` added
(round 103), and `buildStreamCheckpoint` beside its alias (round 104). Alongside it: `@ust-protocol/cli` rc.72,
`@ust-protocol/mcp` rc.31.

| version | round | what closed |
|---------|-------|-------------|
| **protocol rc.41**<br>**#112 closed** | 104 | **`buildCheckpoint` built a STREAM checkpoint three lines from `buildAuthorityCheckpoint`, which names its chain — and the fix is ADDITIVE, so nothing published moved.** `buildStreamCheckpoint` is the name to write; `buildCheckpoint` stays as an alias to the identical function, because removing a published export is a compatibility event and the collision does not require one. The same shape as round 103's `kind: 'fork-choice'`: the ambiguity closes by ADDING what says which, never by taking away what a consumer already imports. **The export SURFACE is now guarded, which is what let this survive:** the prose was checked from round 72 and the wire was never ambiguous, while the API names — where a consumer actually reads — were checked by nothing. Ten exports carry a bare ambiguous term, pinned as a ratchet that may only shrink; a new one fails. **And the qualifier written for PROSE called `buildStreamCheckpoint` unqualified** — `stream ` with a trailing space does not match `build-stream-checkpoint` — so the same rule needed a second form for the second register, caught by a control asserting the code-aware qualifier recognises the very name this round added. **Two gates I built this week then caught my own export:** round 89's COVERAGE demanded it be triaged into a capability, and the R31 partition demanded it be classified. Both correct, both mine, both found within a minute. **And `version-truth` forced the publish earlier than planned:** a new export in the repo and not on npm is a capability gap pinned at zero, and its own text says *publish, or state the policy — do not raise the number*. Published rc.41 rather than raising it, which also means the audit pass ahead of us runs against what a stranger actually installs. |
| **protocol rc.41**<br>**cli rc.73**<br>**#111 closed** | 103 | **An error code sat in the VERDICT slot, and the check covering that seam had pinned the inconsistency instead of objecting to it.** `verifyAsync` and `resolveByDiscovery` returned `{result:'E-MALFORMED'}` from their totality guards, where §15 enumerates the three outcome kinds VALID/INVALID/INDETERMINATE and every other refusal puts the code in `error`. The round-19 P1-02 check asserted **three different shapes for one refusal** across five boundaries — `.error` for two, `.result` for three — which is how the inconsistency survived: the check encoded it. Both verdict guards now use the shape everything else uses, and the check asserts it. **AND THE OWNER'S RULE TURNED A BREAKING DECISION INTO AN ADDITIVE ONE.** I had put two questions to him — rename `forkChoice`'s `result` field, or leave the collision — and he answered with a line instead of a choice: *the verifier always knows where trust was not earned and says so strictly; carry that into the tools, cost regardless*. That rule does not ask for a rename. It asks that a reader never GUESS. So every `forkChoice` return now carries `kind: 'fork-choice'` — a consumer always knows which vocabulary it is reading — and its error codes moved to `error` beside `result: 'REFUSED'`. Nothing was renamed and nothing disappeared: the ambiguity closed additively, which neither of the two options I offered would have done. **The same rule then caught a regression I was about to ship into the tool:** with the codes moved, `ust forkchoice` would have printed a bare `REFUSED` — hiding WHICH trust was not earned, which is the one thing that command exists to say. It prints the code. `E-PREV` and `E-MALFORMED` leave `forkChoiceResults`, and `results` is §14's three again — both measured by spec-code-sync, so neither set can quietly regrow. |

The rule is written into the map's header, because a criterion applied by feel is the thing this whole issue exists to remove. **Applied, it moved three and confirmed eight.** `root-inventory`'s purposes are ANNOTATIONS — the file set is git-derived and checked both ways, so a wrong sentence misleads a reader and lets nothing through — and the map then refused my first grade for it, because the SET comes from `git ls-files` rather than from any source this gate reads, which makes it **1a** and not 2. Hand-typed 11 → 8. |
| **tooling** | 101 | **The list of world properties a ceremony self-check may not condition on named ONE rung of three.** `'authoritative'` was there; `corroborated` and `self-asserted` were not — and both are properties of the world in exactly the same way, since corroboration depends on external witnesses the ceremony neither holds nor should. A self-check conditioning on either would have passed. Neither appears in the CLI today [measured], so nothing was broken; the gap was in the watching, which is now the fourth gate this week where the list was correct and unchecked. **The rung half is now DERIVED from the core's own identity ladder**, so a new rung is forbidden the moment it exists rather than the moment someone remembers this file. Control: a self-check conditioning on `'corroborated'` is caught — a case that was silently legal an hour ago. **The RESOLVER half stays a judgement and says so.** Which call ANSWERS a world question cannot be read off anything: `resolveAuthority`, `noForkConfirmed` and `acceptConsumerOverride` are named with their reasons, and `literal-ok` marks which half is which. That split is the honest shape of this axis — parts of a domain are derivable and parts are decisions, and pretending the second kind away is how a derivation narrows what it was meant to widen. Hand-typed 12 → 11. **Also worth recording: I had declared this axis finished.** I said no derivable domains remained among the twelve, having not opened three of them. Two of the three turned out to have one. |

That is not a reason to keep typing it: a fourth implementation that starts putting a rung in front of a consumer would not be looked at, and this gate exists precisely because a second implementation once minted a verdict word the reference does not have. The roster is now derived — every `.mjs` a package's manifest SHIPS, plus the clean-room verifier and the extension's copy — and a probe file emitting `strength: 'invented-rung'` is caught. **The exemption was strengthened rather than derived, and the difference matters.** `NON_RUNG` cannot be derived from what the code emits without making the check CIRCULAR — the check IS *emitted ⊆ legal*, so deriving `legal` from `emitted` proves nothing. An exemption is exactly where an invented rung would hide. **This is the third time the pattern has repeated** — `display-safety`, `report-shape`, and the strength surfaces are all *the list happens to be complete today* — which is the argument for grading the DOMAIN rather than the outcome: all three were green, all three were correct, and none of them was checked. | **protocol rc.41**<br>**#111 filed** | 86 | **A machine-checkable string set that is not in the canonical REGISTRY becomes a hand-typed roster in whichever gate needs it — the same generator that let a retired op survive in a producer contract for three rounds (round 84).** `verdict-vocabulary-gate` typed `RESULTS` and `COMPLETENESS` itself while every one of those strings lives in the core, and REGISTRY — declared the SINGLE SOURCE OF TRUTH for exactly this kind of set — omitted both. **And the upgrade is the MEASUREMENT, not the move:** relocating a list from a gate to REGISTRY leaves it hand-typed in a different file, so what earns the grade is `spec-code-sync` comparing it against actual code usage. **The moment it was measured, the code disagreed three ways.** `E-MALFORMED` sits in the RESULT slot in the totality guards of `verifyAsync` and `resolveByDiscovery`, where §15 enumerates the three outcome kinds VALID/INVALID/INDETERMINATE and every other refusal puts the code in `error` — it fails CLOSED (`isValid` tests the `VALID:` prefix) and it is a caller-error path, so it is a shape wart rather than a hole, and it is stated that way rather than dressed up. `forkChoice` answers a DIFFERENT question under the same field name — `CANONICAL`, `MULTI_AUTHORITY` — and the two vocabularies OVERLAP at `INDETERMINATE`, so the value cannot even tell a consumer which vocabulary it is in. All three registered as MEASURED sets, which records the collision instead of absorbing it by widening one set to cover the other; #111 carries the decisions, since qualifying a public return field is a breaking change and not mine to make. **MY OWN MEASUREMENT WAS WRONG THREE TIMES BEFORE IT WAS RIGHT, and each failure is one this repo had already recorded.** A temporal dead zone — the check was inserted above the line that declares the source it reads, the same defect that killed the genesis ceremony and was then committed again the same day by the person who fixed it. Reading my OWN COMMENT: the first run reported the core as drifting because it matched `result: 'CANONICAL'` written three lines up inside the REGISTRY comment I had just added — comment-stripping is a lesson `genesis-surface` and `keyop-contract` both learned before me. And a lazy `[\s\S]*?\n}` body match stopped at an inner block and leaked forkChoice's own vocabulary into the verdict set, so the function is now bounded by LINES. **One thing I suspected and it was not true:** I went looking for drift in the pinned completeness roster and found the gate already derives the emitted words from the core and asserts they are all pinned. It was stronger than its grade suggested, and `not_evaluated` lives under a different field name rather than being missed. Result: `verdict-vocabulary` moves 3 → 2 for a real reason, and the assurance map reports it without being told. |

## rc.40 line

Opened because rc.39 is published and a published version is immutable — and because this line removes a
transition rather than adding one, which is a change a reader must be able to find by version.

| version | round | what closed |
|---------|-------|-------------|
| **tooling**<br>**#110** | 85 | **The map of how independent each gate is was a document, and a document goes stale exactly the way the contract that prompted it did.** #110 graded all 56 CI steps by hand: what decides each step's dispute — an external generator (`1a`), two implementations agreeing (`1b`), a domain extracted from the enforcing code (`2`), a hand-typed roster (`3`), or our assertions against our own code (`4`) — with the WEAKER SIDE setting the grade, the same rule the assurance lattice applies to a tier. **Now it is a gate.** The step roster comes from `ci.yml`, the file CI itself executes, so a new step cannot be silently ungraded; each tool DECLARES its grade in its own source beside the code it describes; and the gate refuses a declaration the code contradicts. **Declaration rather than inference, and that was measured rather than assumed:** a first pass that tried to infer graded `root-inventory-gate` as external because it shells out to `git ls-files`, while its real domain is a hand-typed map of filenames. Distinguishing a domain from a fixture is a judgement, so the tool states it and the gate bounds it. **It blocks over-claims ONLY** — declaring weaker than the evidence supports always passes, because a conservative grade is never a lie and a gate that argued for stronger claims would be pointed the wrong way. **AND IT CAUGHT ME TWICE ON ITS FIRST RUN.** `reference-checker.fuzz.mjs` was declared `1a` on the strength of *"pseudorandom inputs decide"*; the case space is a hand-typed list of 19 rule names, so it is `3`. **CORRECTED IN ROUND 97, and the correction is against me twice.** I wrote that there is no randomness in that file at all and that the word appears only in a comment — false: line 52 is `const rng = mulberry32(0xC0FFEE)`, and my grep for `random|seed|LCG` simply did not know the name. The grade was right and BOTH of my stated reasons were wrong. The true one is sharper: the generator is SEEDED WITH A CONSTANT, so the case set is fixed forever — not chance choosing cases, but a choice expressed as a seed. And `temporal-bmc` was declared `1b` for agreeing with an independent abstract model, when that model is written in the SAME FILE by the same hand minutes apart — `2`, since two things one person wrote together are not two implementations. Both were my own grades in #110, written hours earlier. **Result over 57 steps: `1a` 5 · `1b` 5 · `2` 21 · `3` 18 · `4` 7**, 24 declaring a can-fail leg, and a WEAKEST QUADRANT of 16 — domain from memory AND nothing proving the gate can fail. The gate's quadrant is broader than the document's 8 because it counts grade `4` too, which is at least as weak, and because it grades a step by the weakest of ALL tools the step runs rather than by its headline tool. Four negative controls run: an ungraded new step, an over-claimed `1a`, a `canfail:yes` with no leg, a removed marker. **Honest limit, in the header where a reader meets it:** a declaration wrong in a way the evidence does not contradict still passes — so grade ≤2 beside a literal roster demands an explicit `literal-ok:<why this is not the domain>`, which forces a look rather than proving anything. What the gate does prove is that no step is ungraded and none claims a strength its code cannot support. |
